I wrote this with an Intention of showing it to Ferguson, before I sent it off; and accordingly have met with him: But to my great Dissappointment, I learn, that the Project of his succeeding Rouet is absolutely impracticable.†6 The Reasons are not fit to be wrote to you; but the thing is absolutely certain. Let that therefore be no part of your Scheme. I own, that it appears to me that the best Ressource for Ferguson in this Emergency consists in Russel's Friendship; and I know no man who is more capable than he of a generous Action, & of sacrificing his own Interest to that of his Friend. If he insists absolutely upon having this Office, for aught I can see, he must have it: But what a cruel & distressful Situation does it leave poor Ferguson! I beg of you Dear Robertson (and yet I am sure I need not) to employ your Thoughts & Contrivances & good Offices on this Occasion. Let Ferguson be elected instantly, if possible: Delays are infinitely dangerous:†1 And if any Scheme can be afterwards fallen on to provide for him, I can answer for him, that he will allow this Professorship to fall into Russel's hands, for whom indeed it is very well calculated.†2
Letter 31: To SIR ALEXANDER DICK
p 58
†* 31. To SIR ALEXANDER DICK†3
Edinburgh Aug. 26 1760
Dear Sir
I am very much oblig'd to you for allowing me a Perusal of Mr Campbell's†4 Letter, which contains many excellent Remarks, and shows a great Knowlege of the Subject. Above all, I esteem his great Impartiality; a State of Mind, which I have always endeavourd to bring myself to, and which, tho' I hop'd to attain it by my total Freedom from particular Interests and Attachments, I am yet uncertain how far I have been able to reach. The Approbation of so good a Judge as Mr Campbell gives me some Encouragement to think I have not been altogether unsuccessful; and the Anger of both Parties against me, I am also so vain as to think a good Sign.
p 58
I agree with Mr Campbell, that the Charge against Queen Mary is far from resting solely on the Letters: Her public Conduct was so monstrously imprudent, in a multitude of Particulars, that we must conclude, that nothing but Guilt coud lead a Woman of common Sense & common Honour into it. Yet is it material to examine the Authenticity of the Letters; both, because, if genuine, they fix her Character beyond Controversy, and if forg'd, they determine that of her Adversarys, particularly the Earl of Murray, whom otherwise I shou'd incline to think, as the Age went, not a very bad Man. But unhappily, this Fact, which ought to end all Controversy, becomes the Source of fresh Dispute, and I am a little dissappointed to find that Mr Campbell, after reading Dr Robertson's Dissertation,†1 shoud think the Question still doubtful. I ran over the Enquiry,†2 which he mentions; and really found nothing new in it, nor anything capable in the least to shake my former Opinion: This perhaps proceeded from my Opinions being already fixt; and Mr Campbel, being a Neuter, must be the better Judge. The Enquirer's Learning on this Subject seems all to lie within a few Pages of Goodall;†3 and these, according to the usual Artifice of controversial Writers, he has perverted, misrepresented, & curtaild at his Pleasure. It was not surely Goodall, who forc'd Dr Robertson & me, to allow that the French Copy of these Letters was not the Original, but a Translation of a Translation. For the very Title Page of the Book bears it; and it was never conceiv'd to be other wise that I know of. As to the Improbability, that Buchannan, who was an Assistant to the Commissioners at the Conference of York & Hampton-court, woud translate from the Scotch Translation and not from the French Originals themselves, which were in the hand of the Commissioners; this Improbability, I say, is easily solvd. Buchanan might no doubt get a Sight of the Originals; but it is not to be believd, that Murray woud entrust him or any Mortal with the Keeping of them: Murray woud never surely allow them to go out of his own hands; especially as he knew there were false Brethren among the Commissioners, particularly Leddington, who woud have been well-pleas'd to take an Opportunity of destroying them. Besides, can any one doubt, that there were French Letters in Murray's Custody, genuine or forg'd? The Objection is equally strong in either Case, or rather is of no force in both Cases: For the Scotch Translation being taken for just & faithful in the main, it woud suffice well enough for the Ground of another Translation, which servd all the Purposes desird, if it contain'd the Sense of the materiall Passages, as it was only intended to satisfy the Public, & was not meant to be us'd in any judicial Trial.
p 60
I find the Enquirer rests much upon the Tryal of Dalglish, where his carrying the Casket is never mention'd. But Dalgliesh was tryd & punishd for having an active hand in the King's Murther: His carrying the Casket was no Crime & coud not be mentiond on that Occasion. How Morton woud have supported his Narrative of the manner of his intercepting the Casket, is uncertain; but as the Queen recoil'd from the Trial we are left to imagine, that the Proof woud have been full & satisfactory. If Dalgliesh was never askd any Questions about it at no time, the Story woud indeed have been somewhat lame; but surely his Tryal was not the proper time for asking such Questions.
p 60
And here I cannot forbear remarking a curious Controversy between the Enquirer & me. I have said no less than three times, that Queen Mary after Murray gave in his Accusation at Hampton Court refus'd to make any Answer except she was admitted to Queen Elizabeth's Presence: He says, that she offerd to make an Answer, provided she was admitted to that Queens Presence. And for this Difference, which, I fancy, will be undiscernable both to you & Mr Campbel, he very plainly insinuates that I am a Lyar & a Rogue & a Rascal.†1 O! I am happy in my Resolution never to answer to any of these People on any Occasion. For if I had ever been weak enough to have made any Reply to any Remarker, my Silence on this Occasion woud have been taken for a Conviction of Guilt. And how coud I have reply'd? I think it woud scarce have been becoming to have spoke calmly to this Imputation: And had I answer'd a Fool according to his Folly, I must have justly pass'd for a Man of as base a Mind & low an Education as the Person, who coud handle an intricate historical Question in such a manner.
p 61
I have indeed inferd, that Queen Mary knew beforehand, that this Condition, which had been refusd her from the first, woud not now be granted her: But allowing this Inference to be false (and it can only be given as an Inference), a Man is not Rogue and Rascal and Lyar because he draws a false Inference.†1
p 61
There is no Doubt Queen Mary ought to have had Copies of these Letters; and we accordingly find that Q. Elizabeth offers them to her Commissioners, provided she woud promise to answer, without insisting on the Condition already rejected, which surely coud never lead to clear any thing: But she woud not grant Copies that Q. Mary & her Partizans might pick Flaws & make Objections, which Murray & the Scotch Commissioners coud not have an Opportunity of answering: And this Conduct was undoubtedly reasonable.
p 61
I am not altogether of Mr Campbell's Opinion (which I also find to be Dr Robertson's) that Q. Elizabeth never desird that the Controversy shoud go farther. I believe indeed that she woud never have pronouncd any Sentence either for or against the Scotch Queen; but as she undoubtedly believd her guilty (which was also Cecil's & Norfolk's Opinion) she woud have been pleasd to see her non-plus'd and confounded & reducd to Silence; and Mary to prevent this Triumph of her Enemy broke off the Controversy.
p 61
I am sorry to differ from Mr Campbell, who thinks that there is good presumptive Evidence brought against Murray, as having a hand in the King's Murther. Leddington, I believe, had some Share in it; but Leddington was a man of so fickle a Conduct & vary'd his Friendships & Parties so often that his Guilt forms not the slightest Presumption against Murray. I remember, that talking to Dr Robertson, soon after the publishing this Enquiry, I said to him, that I believd Leddington was guilty, and wonderd he had not said so in express Terms in his History. To tell you the Truth, replyd he, my own Book is perhaps the Book in the World which I remember the least, and can the least speak about: For I was so tir'd with writing & correcting & printing it, that I have not now patience to look into it; and ever since it was out of my hands, I have endeavourd to shake off all Thoughts of it. This Sentiment in so fine a Writer I thought curious and remarkable.†1
p 62
As to Morton's Guilt, it is true he was condemnd and sufferd for it; but the Trial is lost, and we know not the Proofs brought against him: And you & Mr Campbell are well enough acquainted with the Iniquity of State Trials during that Age in England as well as in Scotland, to know that a Sentence of Condemnation alone forms not the slightest Presumption of a Man's Guilt.
p 62
We have only Morton's own Account of the Matter, which is so coherent & sensible & agrees so well with the known Facts & Situations of the Persons, (and is also given on or near the Scaffold) that it ought to have considerable Weight with us.
p 62
Besides we are to consider, that tho Morton, sometime before & some time after the Kings Murther, was in Confederacy with Murray; there was at that time a great Coldness, if not Animosity between them. Morton had savd Murray by Rizzios Murther; yet the latter, who was not acquaintd with the Design, deserted his Friend &, after making his own Peace, left him in Banishment without making any Effort in his Favour. It was Bothwel who recalld him, the mortal Enemy of Murray; and Morton naturally fell into a close Union with this new Confederate; at least till some new Interest occurd to separate them.
p 62
The Enquirer insists much on the Maxim Malum†2 minatum & damnum secutum; and because Murray had conspird formerly against Darnely at Beith, infers that he was now his Murtherer; But the Difference or rather Contrariety of the times will not allow that Maxim to be at all apply'd in the present Case. Darnely in the former Period was the Queen's Favourite and had expelld Murray from the Administration, & his Death restord that Nobleman's Power. In the latter Period, he was hated & despisd by the Queen & every Body; & his Death was of no Consequence but as it made her a Widow, & allowd another Person to be admitted as her Husband & Co-partner in the Royalty; a Design, which Murray woud never form in favour of Bothwel.
p 63
Mr Campbel is certainly in the right, in thinking with Dr Robertson, that when such able men as Lord Herrers & Bishop Lesly took a false Step in the Management of the Conference, tho without the Queens Knowlege, it forms a Presumption against her. And he may add, that they persist in the same Measure for six Weeks after, when she was undoubtedly acquainted with it, and concurd in it.
p 63
I shall add to all these Reflections, that if Mary had been innocent, & if these Letters had been Forgeries, she engag'd in the Controversy with great Advantage; as Leddington, the Man of the greatest Penetration in his Age, & who was in all the Secrets of her Enemies, was become secretly her Confederate, & coud have supplyd her with ample Materials for detecting the Forgery. Her declining the Trial is therefore a Presumption, or rather a full Proof of Guilt. I desire Mr Campbel to look to the Passages in the Duke of Norfolk's Trial, to which I refer; and he will find that Lessly & Leddington, as well as Norfolk, always proceed upon the Supposition of Mary's Guilt, in their most secret Conversations with each other.
p 63
I shall not lengthen out a tedious Letter by making any Apologies for it; & shall only desire you to make my Acknowlegements to Mr Campbell for the Permission he has granted me of perusing his Letter. I am Dear Sir
Your most obedient humble Servant
DAVID HUME
p 63
P.S.
I forgot to mention Hubert's Confession, which I say was a regular judicial Paper regularly judicially given in, & ought to have been answerd at the time. It was certainly a judicial Paper, countersignd by a Notary Public, & sent by the Privy Council of Scotland to the Privy Council of England. But I ought not perhaps to have said that it shoud have been answerd at the time; because it was sent up long after the Conferences were ended, and it is likely that Mary & her Commissioners had never heard of it. I have accordingly struck out the last Clause of the Sentence, in case of a second Edition; & shall willingly give this Triumph to the Enquirer; allowing him at the same time to call me, if he pleases, Rogue & Rascal & Lyar for it. I only desire you & Mr Campbel to reflect, that, if these Letters had been forg'd, the Forgers woud never have mentiond Hubert's Name in them; since they must have already had sufficient Experience of his Obstinacy; & it was a Year & a half after, before they coud bring him to a Confession; or rather, according to the Enquirer's Supposition, coud never bring him to any Confession at all, and he dy'd obstinate in maintaining that he never carryd such Letters.†1
Letter 32: To ROBERT CLERK
p 64
†* 32. To ROBERT CLERK†2
[London, 12 Dec. 1761]†3
Dear Clerk
This Forenoon a Chaise was offerd me, which sets out early to morrow Morning, and in which I could go alone to Edinburgh. As I always kept the Purpose of returning, I could not resist this Temptation; tho you & perhaps Lord Shelburn†4 may think my Departure a little abrupt. I must here have Recourse to your Friendship, of which I have had such sensible Experience, both to excuse this Part of my Conduct, and to express to My Lord my full Sense of his Lordships Goodness. I have us'd the Freedom to write to him,†5 but I have not employd the Terms, which give myself Satisfaction on that head, and therefore look for your Assistance to supply my Defects. Surely nothing could be more amiable, more free, more easy, than my Lord's Commerce and Society: Nothing could open better Prospects than the cultivating his Friendship:†1 But these Considerations lose their Influence on a Recluse and an Ascetic, who retains no Ambition, who has lost his Relish for Pleasure, and who is becoming every day unqualify'd for any Pursuit but Sauntering & Study & Indolence. Can the natural Ardour of your Temper allow you to conceive this State of Mind? If it does, make Allowances for it; and believe me, that among the other Advantages, which I renounce by leaving London, your Company is not the one which I shall regreat the least.
p 65
I find our Friend Barré's†2 Deportment yesterday†3 variously descanted on; but the Majority of those I have met with, give it high Commendations. It is certain, that Pitt was confounded; many believe that he will henceforth become a very civil tractable Fellow; some think, that he will appear very little again in the House of Commons. I beg my Compliments to the Man after God's own heart, who has thus struck this Goliah.
I am My Dear Clerk
Yours sincerely
DAVID HUME
Saturday Evening
Letter 33: To BENJAMIN FRANKLIN
p 66
†* 33. To BENJAMIN FRANKLIN†1
〈Edinburgh, 10 May 1762〉
Dear Sir
I have a great many Thanks to give you for your Goodness in remembering my Request, and for the exact Description, which you sent me of your Method of preserving Houses from Thunder. I communicated it to our philosophical Society,†2 as you gave me Permission; and they desire me to tell you that they claim it as their own, and intend to enrich with it the first Collection, [w]hich†3 they may publish.†4 The establish'd Rule of our Society is, that, after a paper is read to them, it is deliverd by them to some Member, who is oblig'd, in a subsequent Meeting, to read some Paper of Remarks upon it. It was communicated to our Friend, Mr Russel;†5 who is pot very expeditious in finishing any Undertaking; and he did not read his Remarks, till the last Week, which is the Reason, why I have been so late in acknowleging your Favour. Mr Russel's Remarks, besides the just Praises of your Invention, contain'd only two Proposals for improving it: One was, that in Houses, where the Rain Water is carry'd off the Roof by a lead Pipe, this metallic Body might be employd as a Conductor to the electric Fire, and save the Expence of a new Apparatus: Another was, that the Wire might be [c]arry'd†3 down to the Foundation of the House, and be thence convey'd below Ground to the requisite Distance, which woud better secure it against Accidents. I thought it proper to convey to you these two Ideas of so ingenious a Man, that you might adopt them, if they appear to you well founded.
p 67
I have sent off your Letter to Lord Mareschal,†1 who will consider himself as much beholden to you. His Lordship is at present very much employ'd in settling the Controversy about the Eternity of Hell-Torments, which has set the little Republic of Neuf-chatel in Combustion.†2 I have ventur'd to recommend to his Lordship the abridging these Torments as much as possible, and have usd the Freedom to employ your Name, as well as my own, in this Request: I have told him, that, as we have taken so much Pains to preserve him & his Subjects from the Fires of Heaven, they cannot do less than to guard us from the Fires of Hell. My Lord told me, when in England, that the King of Prussia could not at first be brought to regard this theological Controversy as a Matter of any Moment, but soon found, from the Confusions, to which it gave rise, that these were not Matters to be slighted. But surely, never was a Synod of Divines more ridiculous, than to be worrying one another, [u]nder†3 the Arbitration of the K. of Prussia & Lord Marischal, who will make an Object of Derision of every thing, that appears to these holy Men so deserving of Zeal, Passion, and Animosity.†4
p 67
I am very sorry, that you intend soon to leave our Hemisphere. America has sent us many good things, Gold, Silver, Sugar, Tobacco, Indigo &c: But you are the first Philosopher, and indeed the first Great Man of Letters for whom we are beholden to her: It is our own Fault, that we have not kept him: Whence it appears, that we do not agree with Solomon, that Wisdom is above Gold: For we take care never to send back an Ounce of the latter, which we once lay our Fingers upon.
p 68
I saw yesterday our Friend Sir Alexander Dick,†1 who desird me to prese[nt]†2 his Compliments to you. We are all very unwilling to think of your settling in America, and that there is some Chance for our never seeing you again: But no-one regrets it more than does Dear Sir
Your most affectionate humble Servant
DAVID HUME
Edinburgh
10 May 1762
Letter 34: To JAMES BOSWELL
p 68
†* 34. To JAMES BOSWELL†3
〈Edinburgh, 24 Feb. 1763〉

You must know, Mr James Boswall or James Boswell Esq, that I am very much out of Humour with you and your two Companions or Co-partners.†4 How the Devil came it into your Heads or rather your Noddles (for if there had been a Head among you, the thing had not happend; nor are you to imagine, that a Parcel of volatile Spirits, enclos'd in a Skull, make a Head) I repeat it, How the Devil came it into your Noddles to publish in a Book to all the World what you pretend I told you in private Conversation?†5 I say, pretend I told you; for as I have utterly forgot the whole Matter, I am resolv'd utterly to deny it.†6 Are you not sensible, that by this Etourderie, to give it the lightest Name, you were capable of making a Quarrel between me and that irascible little man†7 with whom I live in very good Terms? Do you not feel, from your own Experience, that among us, Gentlemen of the Quill, there is nothing of which we are so jealous, not even our Wives, if we have any, as the Honour of our Productions; and that the least Touch of Blame on that head puts us into the most violent Fury and Combustion? I reply nothing to your Letter†1 till you give me some Satisfaction for this offence; but only assure you that I am not Sir
Your most obedient & most humble Servant
DAVID HUME
Edinburgh
24 Feby 1763
Letter 35: To GILBERT ELLIOT OF MINTO
p 69
†* 35. To GILBERT ELLIOT OF MINTO†2
〈Edinburgh, 12 March 1763〉
Dear Sir
In this new Edition,†3 I have corrected several Mistakes & Oversights, which had chiefly proceeded from the plaguy Prejudices of Whiggism, with which I was too much infected when I began this Work. I corrected some of these Mistakes in a former Edition;†4 but being resolv'd to add to this Edition the Quotations & Authorities for the Reigns of James I & Charles I, I was oblig'd to run over again the most considerable Authors who had treated of these Reigns, and I happily discover'd some more Mistakes, which I have now corrected. As I began the History with these two Reigns, I now find that they, above all the rest, have been corrupted with Whig Rancour, and that I really deserv'd the Name of a party Writer, and boasted without any Foundation of my Impartiality: But if you now do me the Honour to give this part of my Work a second Perusal, I am perswaded, that you will no longer throw upon me this reproachful Epithet, and will acquit me of all Propensity to Whiggism. If you still continue to upbraid me, I shall be oblig'd to retaliate on you, and cry, Whig vous-même.
p 70
†1 In page 33, vol 5 you will find a full Justification of the Impositions laid on by James I, without Authority of Parliament; on p. 113, 114, 389, a justification of persecuting the Puritans; in p. 180 a justification of Charles I for levying Tonnage & Poundage with consent of parliament: In p. 100 I acquit James I of prevarication with which I had before rashly charged him. This last mistake, indeed was innocent, and I can easily account for it; I had read Buckingham's Narrative in Rushworth & Franklyn, the two opposite Collectors. I saw what I thought the same paper in the parliamentary History but I did not attend to a line at the bottom, in which it is said that the paper is taken from the Records more full than in the preceding Collection. When I read it lately, I found the Article here quoted. So that this blunder proceeded not from any Spirit of Whiggery.
p 70
I now justify James II more explicitly in his Exercise of the dispensing Power, which was intimately interwove with the Constitution & Monarchy--see vol 6 p. 393, 394, 395, 400. In Volume 4 p. 322, 323, I mention a very remarkable Piece of Tyranny or of Exertion of arbitrary Power practised in that Period, and which came to my Knowledge since the first publication of that Volume.
p 70
There are many other Improvements & Alterations throughout the whole; and I am glad, that Millar has of himself made you an Offer of this Edition. Without flattering you, I must say, that there is no body, whom I more desire to see my Writings as correct as I can make them; and I was thinking to desire Mr Millar to make you this Offer.
p 71
But there is no End of correcting. In this new Edition, vol. 5 p. 205, I have inserted a pretty curious Story of Sir George Markham, which I took from Lord Landesdowne,†1 whom I esteem'd a safe Authority for a Whig Story. But I have since been shown Hobard's Reports,†2 which is infinitely more authentic than Lord Landesdowne, and the Story is there told so as entirely to justify the King & the Star Chamber. So that you may still reproach me, that the villainous Leaven is not entirely purg'd off.
p 71
I am engag'd in no work at present: But if I tire of Idleness, or more properly speaking, of reading for Amusement, I may probably continue my History. My only Discouragement is, that I cannot hope to finish this Work in my Closet, but must apply to the Great for Papers & Intelligence; a thing I mortally abhor.
p 71
Is it not hard & tyrannical in you, more tyrannical than any Act of the Stuarts, not to allow me to publish my Dialogues?†3 Pray, do you not think that a proper Dedication may atone for what is exceptional in them? I am become much of my friend, Corbyn Morrice's†4 Mind, who says, that he writes all his Books for the sake of the Dedications.
p 71
I am very glad to hear from Lord Minto,†5 that you intend to pass a great Part of the ensuing Summer in this Country. Tho' you be now become a great Man,†6 I doubt not but I shoud receive very much Satisfaction from your Society & Conversation: That is, if I be not justled out by Suitors who will press in upon you.
Meanwhile I am Dear Sir
Your affectionate Friend & Servant
DAVID HUME
Edinburgh 12 March 1763
Letter 36: To the REV. HUGH BLAIR
p 72
†* 36. To the REV. HUGH BLAIR†1
London, 6 Oct. 1763
My dear Sir,
I am very glad you have undertaken the task†2 which I used the freedom to recommend to you. Nothing less than what you propose will serve the purpose. You need expect no assistance from Macpherson, who flew into a passion when I told him of the letter I had wrote to you: But you must not mind so strange and heteroclite a mortal, than whom I have scarce ever known a man more perverse and unamiable. He will probably depart for Florida with Governor Johnstone,†3 and I would advise him to travel among the Chickisaws or Cherokees, in order to tame him and civilize him.
p 72
I should be much pleased to hear of the success of your labours. Your method of directing to me is under cover to the Earl of Hertford, Northumberland House. Any letters that come to me under that direction, will be sent over to me at Paris.
p 72
I have no present thoughts of publishing the work you mention; but when I do, I hope you have no objection of my dedicating it to you.†4
p 73
I beg my compliments to Robertson and Jardine. I am very sorry to hear of the state of Ferguson's health. John Home went to the country yesterday with Lord Bute. I was introduced the other day to that Noble Lord, at his desire. I believe him a very good man; a better man than a politician.
p 73
Since writing the above, I have been in company with Mrs Montague,†1 a lady of great distinction in this place, and a zealous partizan of Ossian. I told her of your intention, and even used the freedom to read your letter to her. She was extremely pleased with your project; and the rather, as the Duc de Nivernois,†2 she said, had talked to her much on that subject last winter, and desired, if possible, to get collected some proofs of the authenticity of these poems, which he proposed to lay before the Academie des Belles Lettres at Paris. You see, then, that you are upon a great stage in this enquiry, and that many people have their eyes upon you. This is a new motive for rendering your proofs as complete as possible. I cannot conceive any objection which a man even of the gravest character could have to your publication of his letters, which will only attest a plain fact known to him. Such scruples, if they occur, you must endeavour to remove; for on this trial of yours will the judgment of the public finally depend.
p 73
Lord Bath,†3 who was in the company, agreed with me, that such documents of authenticity are entirely necessary and indispensable.
p 73
Please to write to me as soon as you make any advances, that I may have something to say on this subject to the literati of Paris. I beg my compliments to all those who bear that character at Edinburgh. I cannot but look upon all of them as my friends. I am,
Yours sincerely,
DAVID HUME
6th October 1763.
p 74
I depart hence in eight days.†1
Letter 37: To WILLIAM ROBERTSON
p 74
†* 37. To WILLIAM ROBERTSON
〈Paris, 1 Dec. 1763〉
Dear Robertson
Among other agreeable Circumstances, which attend me at Paris, I must mention that of having a Lady for a Translator, a Woman of Merit, the Widow of an Advocate.†2 She was before very poor and known but to few; but this Work has got her Reputation and has procurd her a Pension from the Court, which sets her at her Ease. She tells me, that she has got a Habit of Industry; and wou'd continue, if I coud point out to her any other English Book she cou'd undertake, without running the Risque of being anticipated by any other Translator. Your History of Scotland is translated and is in the Press: But I recommended to her, your History of Charles V, and promis'd to write to you, in order to know when it wou'd be printed and to desire you to send me over the Sheets from London as they came from the Press: I shoud put them into her hands, and she woud by that means have the Start of every other Translator. My two Volumes last publish'd are at present in the Press. She has a very easy natural Style: Sometimes she mistakes the Sense; but I now correct her Manuscript; and shou'd be happy to render you the same Service, if my Leizure permit me, as I hope it will.
p 74
Do you ask me about my Course of Life? I can only say, that I eat nothing but Ambrosia, drink nothing but Nectar, breathe nothing but Incense, and tread on nothing but Flowers. Every Man I meet, and still more every Lady, wou'd think they were wanting in the most indispensible Duty, if they did not make to me a long & elaborate Harangue in my Praise. What happend last Week,†1 when I had the Honour of being presented to the Dauphin's Children at Versailles, is one of the most curious Scenes I have yet pass'd thro. The Duc de Berry,†2 the eldest, a boy of ten Years old, stept forth, and told me, how many Friends & Admirers I had in this Country, and that he reckond himself in the Number, from the Pleasure he had receivd by the reading of many Passages in my Works. When he had finish'd, his Brother, the Count de Provence,†3 who is two Years younger, began his Discourse, and informd me, that I had been long & impatiently expected in France; and that he himself expected soon to have great Satisfaction from the reading of my fine History. But what is more curious; when I was carry'd thence to the Count d'Artois,†4 who is but five Years of Age, I heard him mumble something, which, tho' he had forgot it in the way, I conjectur'd, from some scatterd Words, to have been also a Panegyric dictated to him. Nothing cou'd more Surprize my Friends, the Parisian Philosophers, than this Incident:†5 For the Duke de Vauguion,†6 the Governor & the Countess de Marsan,†7 the Governess, have that Character, that a Panegyric on such a Fellow as Bumbo†8 or Sandy Webster†9 was more to be expected from them than one on me. Whence it is conjecturd that this Honour was pay'd me by express Orders from the Dauphin, who indeed is not, on any Occasion, sparing in my Praise.
p 76
All this Attention and Panegyric was at first oppressive to me; but now it sits more easy. I have recoverd, in some measure, the Use of the Language, and am falling into Friendships, which are very agreeable; much more so than silly, distant Admiration. They now begin to banter me and tell droll Stories of me; which they have either observd themselves or have heard from others; so that you see I am beginning to be at home. It is probable, that this place will be long my home. I feel little Inclination to the factious Barbarians of London; and have ever desir'd to remain in the Place where I am planted. How much more so, when it is the best Place in the World? I cou'd here live in great Abundance on the half of my Income: For there is no place, where Money is so little requisite to a Man who is distinguishd either by his Birth or by personal Qualities. I cou'd run out you see in a Panegyric on the People; but you woud suspect, that this was a mutual Convention between us. However, I cannot forbear observing, on what a different Footing Learning & the Learned are here, from what they are among the factious Barbarians abovemention'd.
p 76
I have here met with a prodigious historical Curiosity, the Memoirs of King James II, in fourteen Volumes, all wrote with his own hand, and kept in the Scots College.†1 I have lookd into it, and have made great Discoveries. It will be all communicated to me; and I have had an Offer of Access to the Secretary of State's Office, if I want to know the Dispatches of any French Minister that resided in London. But these Matters are much out of my head. I beg of you to visit often Lord Marischal†2 who will be pleasd with your Company. I have little Paper remaining & less time; and therefore conclude abruptly by assuring you, that I am Dear Dr Yours sincerely
DAVID HUME
Paris,
1 Decr 1763
Letter 38: To COLONEL JAMES EDMONSTOUNE
p 77
†* 38. To COLONEL JAMES EDMONSTOUNE
〈Paris, 9 Jan. 1764〉
Dear Edmondestone
I was fully settled, and as I thought for Life, at Edinburgh: Had bought a very pretty little House,†1 which I had repair'd and furnishd to my Fancy: Had purchas'd a Chaise, and fix'd every thing about my Family, on such a Footing as to continue there the rest of my Days: But while I was in this Situation, which was far from disagreeable, I receivd a Letter from My Friend, Mr Wood,†2 wrote by Directions from Lord Hertford,†3 by which I was invited to attend his Lordship in his Ambassy to Paris and to perform the Functions of Secretary to the Ambassy. I had never seen Lord Hertford; tho I had heard an excellent Character of him: But as I thought myself too old to enter on a new Scene of Life, and found myself settled to my Mind, I at first refus'd the Invitation; but on its being urg'd more home to me, I came up to London; where I found that Mr Bunbury,†4 a Gentleman of considerable Fortune & marry'd to the Duke of Richmond's Sister, had already been appointed Secretary, but was so disagreeable to the Ambassador that he was resolvd never to see nor do Business with his Secretary and therefore desird I shoud attend him in order to perform the Functions. He also thought himself certain that Bunbury coud not possibly continue in this Situation; but in order to make me more secure, he procurd me a Pension of 200 a Year for Life from the King. As I became every day better acquainted with my Lord, I likd him every day better; and I do not believe there is in the World a man of more probity & Humanity, endowd with a very good Understanding, and adornd with very elegant Manners & Behaviour. My Lady is also a Person of great Merit, and nothing can be more amiable than my Lord Beauchamp;†1 so that you see, I have every domestic means of Happiness; and the good Reception I have met with at Paris, particularly, as you observe, by the Ladies, renders my present Course of Life, tho' somewhat too much hurry'd and dissipated, as amusing as I coud wish. My Lord appears zealously my Friend; and has urgd the Matter so home in my favour to the King & the Ministers, that he has obtaind a Promise, that I shall soon have the Appointments & Commission of Secretary to the Embassy which is about 1000 a Year, added to what I possess already. So that you see, Dear Edmondestone, I am in the high Road to Riches; and as there is no Instance of a Secretary to the Embassy of Paris, that has not been advancd to the most considerable Employments, I am at the same time in the high Road to Dignities. You must know, that Lord Hertford has so high a Character for Piety, that his taking me by the hand is a kind of Regeneration to me; and all past Offences are now wip'd off. But all these Views are trifling to one of my Age and Temper: The material Point is, (if anything can be material) that I keep my Health & my Humour as entire as I possessd them at five & twenty. I am sorry to hear, Dear Edmondestone, that the Case is not the same with you, at least with regard to the former: And perhaps somewhat, with regard to the latter. Your Situation is no doubt tiresome, & sometimes†2 disagreeable.†3 What is the Fancy of sending one of the first Noblemen in the Kingdom to pass Years in a Country-Town? Why do you not go forward to Italy or come back to Paris?
p 78
When I arriv'd here, all M Voltaire's Friends told me of the Regard he always express'd for me; and they perswaded me, that some Advances on my part were due to his Age & woud be well taken. I accordingly wrote him a Letter,†4 in which I expressd the Esteem which are [sic] undoubtedly due to his Talents; and among other things I said, that, if I were not confind to Paris by public Business, I shou'd have a great Ambition to pay him a Visit at Geneva. This is the Foundation of the Report you mention:†1 But I am absolutely confind to Paris & the Court and cannot on any account leave them for so much as three Days.
p 79
†2 Just before I left Scotland, I passd some days at Blair, where I saw your House from the other Side of the Water.†3 It appears to me a very agreeable Situation: But the House will be a little costly. Write me soon & longer. I wrote you a long Letter†4 from Edinburgh, which I hope came to hand. I sent it under Cover to Sir Harry.†5 I am too great Politician to write any News. Have you read the two last, first, Volumes of my History in English?

Paris. 9th January 1764
To Collonel Edmondestone.
Letter 39: To JEAN-BAPTISTE-ANTOINE SUARD
p 79
†* 39. To JEAN-BAPTISTE-ANTOINE SUARD†6
[Paris, Nov. 1763-March 1764]†7
Vous m'avez fait, Monsieur, un vrai plaisir de m'informer de l'arrivée de Mlle de Langlotierre. Je me rendrais auprès de vous environ les cinques heures et demi, cet après-dînée, et vous aurez la bonté de me mener chez elle, sans la [sic] rien dire. Pour dîner ou souper chez elle, il me sera presque impossible, étant engagé pour toute la semaine. Si l'heure que je prens la liberté de vous donner ne vous convienne pas, je vous prie de ne vous gêner en aucune façon, parce que la même heure demain me conviendrait également. Je serai charmé si, au même tems que je renouvelle une ancienne connaissance, je pourrai ajouter encore au nombre de mes amis une personne comme vous, si recommandable en toute façon et dont je fais un grand cas.
p 80
J'ai l'honneur d'être, Monsieur, votre très humble et très obéissant serviteur,
DAVID HUME
Hôtel de Grimberg,†1 lundi 10 heures, le matin.
Letter 40: To CATHERINE MACAULAY
p 80
†* 40. To CATHERINE MACAULAY†2
〈Paris, 29 Mar. 1764〉
Madam,
The agreeable present†3 which you was so good as to make me, did not come to hand till a few days ago; it had been packed up with some of Lord Hertford's baggage, and was so long on the road: I should not otherwise have been so long wanting to express my thanks for the pleasure your performance has given me;†1 and also for the obliging manner in which you mention me, even when you oppose my sentiments. I find, indeed, that you often do me the honor to keep me in your eye, during the course of your narration; and I flatter myself that we differ less in facts, than in our interpretation and construction of them. Perhaps also I have the misfortune to differ from you in some original principles, which it will not be easy to adjust between us.†2 For as I look upon all kinds of subdivision of power, from the monarchy of France to the freest democracy of some Swiss Cantons, to be equally legal, if established by custom and authority; I cannot but think, that the mixed monarchy of England, such as it was left by Queen Elizabeth, was a lawful form of government, and carried obligations to obedience and allegiance; at least it must be acknowledged, that the princes and ministers who supported that form, tho' somewhat arbitrarily, could not incur much blame on that account; and that there is more reason to make an apology for their antagonists than for them. I grant, that the cause of liberty, which you, Madam, with the Pyms and Hampdens have adopted, is noble and generous; but most of the partizans of that cause, in the last century disgraced it, by their violence, and also by their cant, hypocrisy, and bigotry, which, more than the principles of civil liberty, seem to have been the motive of all their actions. Had those principles always appeared in the amiable light which they receive both from your person and writings, it would have been impossible to resist them; and however much inclined to indulgence towards the first James and Charles, I should have been the first to condemn those monarchs for not yielding to them. But lest you think that the air of this place has infected me with the style of gallantry, I beg leave to conclude, by expressing my great esteem of your history, and my great personal respect for yourself. I beg my compliments to the doctor; and am, Madam, with great sincerity,
Your most obedient humble servant,
DAVID HUME
Paris, 29th March,
1764.
Letter 41: To COLONEL JAMES EDMONSTOUNE
p 82
†* 41. To COLONEL JAMES EDMONSTOUNE
[Paris, April 1764]
Dear Edmondestone,
I was just projecting to write a long Letter to you and another to Mr Vivian,†1 when your last obliging Epistle†2 came to hand. I immediatly put Pen to Paper, to assure you, that the Report is entirely groundless, and that I have not lost nor ever coud have lost a Shilling by Fairholm's Bankruptcy.†3 Poor John Adams†4 is very deeply engagd with him, and was at first believd to be ruin'd: But I had a Letter last Post from Dr Blair, which informs me that he will yet be able to save 15 or 16000 Pounds. I am glad to give you also this Piece of Intelligence.
p 82
What! do you not know, that Lord Bute†5 is again all-powerful, or rather, that he was always so; but is now acknowledg'd for such by all the World? Let this be a new Motive for Mr Vivian to adhere to the ecclesiastical Profession, in which he may have so good a Patron: For civil Employments for Men of Letters are scarcely to be found: All is occupyd by Men of Business, or by Parliamentary Interest.
p 83
It is putting too great a Respect on the Vulgar, and on their Superstitions, to pique one'self on Sincerity with regard to them. Did ever one make it a point of Honour to speak Truth to Children or Madmen? If the thing were worth being treated gravely, I shoud tell him, that the Pythian Oracle, with the approbation of Xenophon, advisd every one to worship the Gods {nomöi poleös}. I wish it were still in my Power to be a Hypocrite in this particular: The common Duties of Society usually require it; and the ecclesiastical Profession only adds a little more to an innocent Dissimulation or rather Simulation, without which it is impossible to pass thro the World. Am I a Lyar, because I order my Servant to say I am not at home, when I do not desire to see company.
p 83
How coud you imagine that I was Under-Secretary to Lord Hertford, or that I cou'd ever be prevail'd on to accept of such a Character?†1 I am not Secretary at all but do the Business of Secretary to the Ambassy, without any Character. Bunbury has the Commission and Appointments; a young man of three or four and twenty; somewhat raw and ignorant, whom Lord Hertford refus'd to accept of, as thinking he woud be of no Use to him. The King gave me a Pension of 200 a year for Life to engage me to attend his Lordship. My Lord is very impatient to have me Secretary to the Embassy, and writes very earnest Letters to that Purpose to the Ministers, and among the rest, to Lord Bute. He engagd me, somewhat against my Will, to write also to such of my Friends as had Credit with that Favorite, Oswald, Elliot, Sir Harry and John Hume: The King has promisd that my Lord Hertford shall soon be satisfyd in this particular; and yet, I know not how, I suspect that some Obstacle will still interpose. Tho' nothing can be more scandalous than for a man to enjoy the Revenue of an Office, which is exercisd by another; Mr Bunbury has great Interest, being marryd to a Sister of the Duke of Richmond and Sister in law to Lord Holland. The Appointments of this Office are above 1000 a Year, and the Expense attending it nothing and it leads to all the great Employments. I wait the Issue with Patience and even with Indifference: At my Years and with my Fortune a man with a little common Sense, without Philosophy may be indifferent about what happens.
I am Dear Edmondestone,
Yours sincerely,
DAVID HUME.
Letter 42: To MARQUISE DE BARBENTANE
p 84
†* 42. To the MARQUISE DE BARBENTANE†1
〈Paris, 8 June 1764〉

I flatter myself, Madam, that I have found a sure and a safe Opportunity of commissioning from London the Books,†2 which you did me the Honour to mention to me: But before I can do it with the requisite Exactness, I believe, that it will be necessary for me to see Monsr Du Pons.†3 If you desire, therefore, that the Books shou'd be commissioned soon, you must be so good as to make that Gentleman allow me an Interview with him: If you be in no Hurry, we may delay it till I meet him at Compiegne.†4 I have been this Evening to pay my Respects to you, and to wish you a good Journey. And I have the Honour to be with the greatest Regard
Madam
Your most obedient humble Servant
DAVID HUME
Hotel de Brancas†5
8th of June 1764
p 84
Lord Hertford goes to Compiegne the 25th or 26th of the Month, and I accompany him thither.

A Madame
Madame La Comtesse de Barbantane
Letter 43: To COLONEL ISAAC BARRÉ
p 85
†* 43. To COLONEL ISAAC BARRÉ
Compiègne 16 of July 1764.
Dear Collonel
For you are still a Collonel, notwithstanding your Fall,†1 and on that Footing it is allowable for me to entertain a Correspondence with you: I repeat it then, Dear Collonel, and I say it with Truth, whether you be a Collonel or not,†2 that I am very sorry, I was not in Paris, when you arriv'd in that City, the Center of Arts, of Politeness, of Gallantry, and of good Company. I shoud have rejoic'd to have embrac'd you and to have congratulated you on your arrival in your native Country.†3 I am also sorry, that you propose to remain so little time in Paris, that I cannot propose to see you there, on your first Appearance. I hope, on your Return, to meet you, and to make myself Amends for the present Dissappointment.
p 85
I suppose the Marigny you mean, is Monsr Marigny,†4 Brother to Madame Pompadour,†1 who dyd in Spring last,†2 and carryd with her all the Credit and Authority and Reputation, tho' not all the Money, of her Brother. The Courtiers at present begin to recollect that he is a person of mean Birth and low Education; a Fact which they had forgot during the twenty Years preceding; and they now turn their Backs upon him with equal Care as formerly they met him with open and smiling and caressing Faces: For such are the Manners, My Dear Collonel, of your native Country. You know, that we, on the other Side of the Water, make no Difference whether a Man be in Credit or not, possess Power or not, be in Office or not, be a Governor or not: Nothing but personal Merit is regarded: Past Professions of Friendship are inviolable: Virtue and public Good are held alone in estimation: &c &c.
p 86
With regard, then, to Monsr Marigny, I know not who coud tell you, that his Recommendation coud be of use to you in your Journey thro' the Provinces of France. When I first arrivd in France, as I was a Man in Vogue and he a Man in Power, he made me some fine flourishing Speeches, and I made him some awkard [sic] Bows; and our Correspondence did not go much farther. But the Case is, at present, he is neither at Compiegne nor at Paris: He is retird to a Seigneurie, about sixty Leagues Distance, which was left him by his Sister; and in that place or in some other, he will pine away the rest of his Life, full of Regret, that the Dukes and Mareschals and Blue Ribbons, who formerly caress'd him, do now conspire to neglect him; and all his Riches will be of no Avail to give him any Enjoyment. Put not your Trust, Dear Collonel, in Princes nor in the Sons of Men: For in them there is no Salvation.†3
p 86
I do not mean, but you might have safely put your Trust in me, had it been in my Power to serve you: I coud easily have given or procurd you recommendations to Marigny: Vaille qui vaille.†4 But you see, by the present Situation of Affairs, that it is impracticable, and I believe that you have not much reason to regreat the want of them. You will make your way by means of Gold and Silver--and Brass, thro all the Towns of France. I wish, however, I knew in what Towns you proposd chiefly to reside: For it might not be impossible for me to procure you an Introduction, which your personal Merit wou'd afterwards improve to your Advantage and to the future Advantage of your adopted Country.
p 87
Pray, in what sphere does that Meteor, Clarke,†1 move at present? What excentric Motions, more excentric than any in the Ptolemaic System, does he form? Is he any wise regulated by the Newtonian Theory, or is sensible of any Attraction to the Hotel de Beaupréau?†2 But these and a hundred other idle Questions, I propose to put to you on your Return.--Lord Hertford was in Paris when you wrote me. I hope you waited on him or will wait on Lady Hertford and Lord Beauchamp.†3--I wish you good Weather, good Health, a good Journey, and a safe Return: And so I bid you heartily adieu: I have not room to make a bow; but can only Shake you by the hand.
DAVID HUME
Letter 44: To [ ]
Hume: NLDH Ltr 44 To: ?? [64] p 87
†* 44. To [ ]†4
〈Paris, 7 Oct. 1764〉
Sir
In Compliance with Lord Halifax's†5 Desire, an Application was made by Lord Hertford in favour of Monsr Calvet†6 last Tuesday, the first day that his Excellency had occasion to see the French Minister after I was honour'd with yours of the 15th of September. Monsr de Praslin†1 said that, if it were possible, the Laws against Refugees shoud be dispens'd with in Monsr Calvet's Case, and he shoud have leave to sell his Land. I immediatly calld on Monsr Calvet, in order to communicate to him this Intelligence, and to concert with him the Means of rendering his Desire effectual; But found, that he had set out for Gascogny two days before. He is expected to return in six Weeks,†2 at which time, I shall endeavour to serve him by all means in my power; being pleas'd with any Opportunity, by which I can prove how much I am
Sir
Your most obedient and most humble Servant
DAVID HUME.
Paris.
7th of Octr 1764
Letter 45: To DR. WILLIAM HUNTER
p 88
†* 45. To [DR. WILLIAM HUNTER]†3
〈Fontainebleau, 24 Oct. 1764〉
Dear Sir
Immediatly upon receiving yours†4 of the 21st of August, I waited on Dr Astruc,†5 and left with him your Letters, together with a Card for myself. I expected everyday, before I left Paris, that he woud have return'd my Visit, when I might have had some Conversation with him on the Subject of your Letter. I delay'd acknowleging your Favour, till I coud be able to inform you of something more particular on that head. But as I never had an Opportunity of seeing the Doctor, I am not able to say any thing farther, than to assure you, that your Letter was deliver'd to him.
p 89
All this Family†1 are well; but some of us in great Distress for the Departure†2 of Lord Beauchamp, which indeed we have, all of us, great reason to regreat. I am
Dear Sir
Your most obedient and most humble Servant
DAVID HUME
Fontainebleau
24 Octr 1764
Letter 46: To H. M. SECRETARY OF STATE
p 89
†* 46. To [H. M. SECRETARY OF STATE]†3
Compiegne 28th of July 1765
Sir
In obedience to your Excellency's Commands of the 14th Instant, which I receiv'd not till the 25th, I went to the Duke of Praslin, and declar'd to him, conformable to your Excellency's Instructions,†4 that His Majesty, tho' he had chang'd his Administration at home, persever'd still in the same Disposition with regard to the several Powers in Alliance with him abroad, and that His Intention and Inclination were to maintain and preserve inviolable the Peace lately concluded, and to cultivate and improve the Harmony thereby happily establish'd. The Duke of Praslin made me the same Declaration on the Part of his most Christian Majesty.
p 90
Yesterday, I receiv'd a Visit from the Count de Bernis,†1 Nephew to the Cardinal,†2 who apply'd to me in his own Name and in that of his Uncle. The Object of their Application was this: Lately, the King of France had sent against the Sallee†3 Rovers a small Squadron, consisting of a few Frigates, which were so happy as to execute the Object of their Expedition. By means of Boats, which they sent up the River, they burnt, at Harache [sic],†4 some Privateers of that Nation; but in this Action, it unfortunately happen'd, that the Chevalier de Narbonne,†5 a young Man of Quality, Nephew to the Cardinal and Brother in law to the Count, was wounded and taken Prisoner by the Moors. His Relations in France are much at a Loss how to convey to him any Assistance and to make a Bargain for his Ransom; and they think, that the readiest Means is to have Recourse to the Friendship and Generosity of the English Nation. They are inform'd, that His Majesty has a Consul†6 at Sallee (a Point that I neither could contradict nor confirm) and they are willing to hope, that your Excellency, if acquainted with the Case, wou'd convey Orders to him, to give all manner of Protection and perform every kind of good Office to the young Gentleman, who must at present stand so much in need of Assistance. They also wish, that he wou'd conclude some Bargain with the Moors, for the Ransom of the Chevalier; and as soon as they are informd of the Terms, they will take care to remit the Money to the Consul, in any Method which he will direct, together with such Money as he might otherwise have expended in assisting the Prisoner. They think, that, in this Transaction, it were better, the Moors did not know any thing farther of the Quality of the Person, except that he was Ensign of the Terpsichore; for such is the Name of the Frigate to which he belongs. If His Majesty has no Consul at Sallee, they flatter themselves, that any other English Consul on that Coast or the Governor of Gibraltar,†1 may, from your Excellency's Instructions, be willing to perform this good Office. I have us'd the Freedom of giving you a Detail of this Affair; being sensible, that your Excellency wou'd gladly lay hold of an Occasion, both of performing an Act of Humanity and of obliging so many Persons of Distinction in this Country.†2
p 91
I take this Opportunity, Sir, of congratulating you on the late Mark which you have receiv'd of His Majesty's Favour, and of recommending myself to your Excellency's Protection. I have the Honour to be, with the greatest Esteem and Regard Sir
Your Excellency's
Most obedient & most humble Servant
DAVID HUME.
Mr Secretary Conway
Letter 47: To H. M. SECRETARY OF STATE
p 91
†* 47. To [H. M. SECRETARY OF STATE]
Compiegne 12 of August 1765
Sir
I was honour'd with your Excellency's of the 5th of August on Thursday the 8th late in the Evening by Roworth the Messenger. Next day, I desir'd an Audience of M. de Praslin, which was granted me on the subsequent day. M. de Guerchy†1 was in company with him. I touch'd successively on all the Points, recommended to me by your Excellency.
p 92
On the head of Dunkirk,†2 M. de Praslin confirm'd to me, that the Agreement which M. de Guerchy had made with your Excellency shou'd be carry'd into Execution, viz, that the French shou'd instantly begin the Destruction of the Jettees erected in the Harbour of Dunkirk, that they shou'd proceed gradually in the Work, that they shou'd carefully observe the Effects of that Operation; and if the Result was to lessen the Depth of Channel, without preventing the Outlet of the Backwater; in that Case, the Object of both Courts was happily attain'd; the Harbour of Dunkirk wou'd be no longer formidable, and the Country behind wou'd still be preserv'd dry and healthy. But at the same time, he inculcated on me to remind your Excellency, that, if the Result shou'd prove otherwise; if, as was suspected, the filling up of the Channel prevented the Outlet of the Waters; in that Case, it was understood between the two Courts, not only that the French shou'd stop in the Destruction of the Jettees, but also that they might rebuild what they had destroy'd till some other Expedient cou'd be fallen on to drain the Country. I reply'd, that in such an Event the Expedient for that Purpose was obvious; it was the same which Lewis the 14th had put in Practice, and which had prov'd effectual during the Course of 40 Years, viz, the opening the Canal of Mardyke, and shutting the Sluice of Bergues. M. de Praslin answer'd, that this Expedient requir'd time to be carry'd into Execution; and it was not fitting, that, in the mean while a whole Province shou'd be utterly destroy'd. But he also beg'd of me not to imagine, that the Expedient I mention'd had ever in reality been effectual, or that the Country, during these forty Years, had ever been healthy. The Quarters of Dunkirk, he cou'd assure me, was then always avoided, as much as possible, by all the Officers, as destructive to their own Health and to that of the Soldiers; but at present Dunkirk was as healthy as Compiegne; and no Objections were ever made to the quartering there.
p 93
Our Conversation pass'd next to the Case of the Canada Bills;†1 where M. de Praslin acknowleg'd, that it was the Intention of his Court to pay the Cards and Billets d'Ordonnance in two or three Years: He cou'd not at present promise it ministerially nor precisely, till he had consulted with the Controler General: And in order to enable him to do so, he wish'd that we cou'd give him, as near as possible, the Amount of these Billets: For that a different Arrangement wou'd be requisite in proportion to the different Amount of the Demand. I told him, that, by the Accounts transmitted to me, he might safely take eleven Millions as being nearly the Amount of the Billets d'Ordonnance, and might make his Arrangement accordingly.
p 93
He also told me, that the Intention of his Court was to put the unregister'd Billets on the same footing with the register'd; but this Concession, he said, was loaded with a Condition, that the Proprietors shou'd declare their Acquiescence in the Liquidation of 25 per cent prompt Payment. As the Duke did not mention to me any Plan for their making this Declaration, it may probably be nothing but a general Menace thrown out, in order to intimidate them and make them more tractable.
p 93
I next open'd to him your Excellency's Demand of putting the Billets d'Ordonnance on the same Footing with the Bills drawn in 1759; a Proposal, I told him, which your Excellency was not authoriz'd by the Proprietors to make, but which you only hop'd they wou'd acquiesce in. He ask'd me, upon what Calculation or Principles of Reasoning you made that Demand. I us'd the Freedom to ask him in return, upon what Principle the Court of France had made the Reduction; if it was either on account of the Frauds committed by their own Officers, or on account of the low Price at which our Merchants had bought those Billets, I thought that nothing cou'd be more easy than to prove both of these Reasons to be insufficient. No! reply'd he, we insist on neither of these Topics: We alledge only the insufficient Value given for them by the first Proprietors, on account of the great Discredit into which they had fallen. Upon that Principle, answerd I, I think it easy to prove that the Demand of my Court is just and equitable. First a great Part of those Billets were issu'd preceding the Draught of Bills in 1759, and consequently are intitled to the same Liquidation as those Bills. Secondly Even in 1760 a great Part of them was issu'd in Payment of Corn and Cattle, which were tax'd at a low Value by the Governor and Intendant.
p 94
To prove the first Point, I told him, that it was impossible these Billets cou'd all of them have been converted into Bills of Exchange; because many of them were for very low Sums, such as 30 or 40 Sous; because, in so wide and wild a Country, it was not practicable for every Planter or Day-labourer to find means of sending his Billets to the Intendant's Office; because many of the Planters wou'd have no Use for Bills on Europe; and because the Intendant had shut up his Office and refus'd Bills to many, which had oblig'd the Proprietors of the Billets to depart to their several homes; and tho' afterwards the Cry of the Country had forc'd him again to open his Office, yet People at a Distance, either were unacquainted with this Change of Resolution or found it impracticable to return.
p 94
The Duke reply'd, that those small Sums I mention'd were Cards not Billets, and cou'd only amount to a mere Trifle; that as the Conversion of Billets into Bills on Europe was for many Years an annual and regular Operation, all the Planters had by Practice learn'd the Method of transmitting their Billets to the Intendant; that the Colony was always in general very much in Debt to the Mother Country, so that those who had no Demands of their own on Europe, easily found others who took their Bills from them on advantageous Terms; and that, tho' he acknowleg'd the Fact, of the Intendant's once shutting up his Office, yet he insisted, that, on opening of it again, most of the Planters found means to have their Billets presented, and to receive Bills in return.
p 94
However, added he, we do not absolutely assert, that there remain'd in the hands of the Canadians no Billets prior to the Draught in 1759. We believe there were some, but I am inform'd they are of small Amount. It is a particular Hardship and Injustice, which we ought to redress, and which we shall redress. M. de Fontanieu,†1 who was at the head of the Commission for forming this Liquidation has assur'd me, that the Operation wou'd be as easy as it was equitable. I told him, added the Duke, that you propos'd to have a Conversation with him; he said that he desir'd nothing more; but he rather wish'd that it shou'd be at Paris than at Compiegne; in the former Place, he was in his Office, and cou'd produce to you authentic Documents of whatever he shou'd advance; he doubted not, but he cou'd prove to you or any reasonable Man, by the most irrefragable Arguments, that the Billets d'Ordonnance, had, in proportion to the State of the Case, been treated by our Arrets with even more Indulgence than the Bills of Exchange in 1759, with which you wou'd endeavour to put them on a Level.
p 95
I intend, on my return to Paris, which will be in a few days, to wait on M. de Fontanieu, and endeavour to draw from him some Lights on so intricate a Subject. I shall afterwards transmit to your Excellency the Result of our Conversation.†1
p 95
When I proceeded to the second Topic, viz, the low Taxation of Corn and Cattle in 1760, both the Duke of Praslin and M. de Guerchy absolutely deny'd that there ever had been any such Taxation.†2 I ask'd the Duke, whether, in case he found upon Enquiry, that there had been such a Taxation (which there certainly was) whether I say, he wou'd engage also to remedy this particular Injustice. He said, that in a Case so much unknown to him, he was unwilling to take any Engagements: But be assur'd, said he, that we desire nothing but to do you Justice, and even to show you Favour. I am asham'd to say, that we here show you more favour than even to our own Subjects. We consent to grant prompt Payment to such Billets d'Ordonnance as are in the hands of the English; while we post-pone the Payment of the Billets, which are in the hands of the French. We make this Concession for the Sake of public Peace; and in order to establish a mutual Confidence between the Nations. We hope, that you will not be insensible of our fair Proceedings in this particular; and that we shall not meet with a double Difficulty, both of justifying ourselves to you and to our own People.
p 96
After this Conversation was over, without my being able to gain any thing upon him, because he always refer'd me to M. de Fontanieu, I next demanded, that Browne,†1 the Innkeeper at Dunkirk, shou'd be restor'd to his Liberty and shou'd be allow'd to settle in Dunkirk. The first Demand he willingly comply'd with; but the second, notwithstanding the plain Sense of Treaties, I found in him no Disposition to agree to.
p 96
Having thus deliver'd to your Excellency an Account of my Conference with the Duke of Praslin and the Count de Guerchy, I proceed to such other Matters as His Majesty may desire to be acquainted with.
p 96
About two Months ago, Lord Hertford convey'd Intelligence to His Majesty, that a good Understanding was beginning between the Courts of Versailles and Berlin; at least, that the King of Prussia had made Advances towards renewing his Correspondence with France, and that this Court seemd very willingly to embrace the Overture. I can now inform your Excellency that the Negotiation is very far advanc'd; that there is a Correspondence of Letters already open'd between the two Courts; and that each will soon have a Minister resident with the other. It is not thought however that this Step will immediatly lead to any farther Consequences, except to preserve Decency between two such great Courts; nor do I find, that the Imperial Ambassadeur takes any Umbrage at it. The King of France has a personal and a strong Aversion to the King of Prussia; of which the latter is very sensible, as I have been inform'd by a Gentleman,†2 who lives in great Familiarity with him. For he one day express'd to this Gentleman his Wonder whence that Antipathy cou'd arise; since he profess'd, that in all Transactions there had never any thing occurd which cou'd give the least personal Disgust to His most Christian Majesty.
p 96
The Clergy are at present assembled at Paris. As the Authority of the Crown is somewhat low in France, all public Bodies are apt to become troublesome; and for that Reason great Care was taken in the Election of Members to this Assembly. Yet, notwithstanding this Precaution, the Assembly, near three Weeks ago, voted unanimously an Address†1 to the King of a very extraordinary Nature. It consisted of seven Articles: They desir'd, that the Arret, which enjoin'd Silence with regard to the Bull unigenitus shou'd be recall'd; that the Reception of the Bull shou'd be declar'd an Article of Faith; that whoever refus'd to receive it shou'd not be admitted to the Sacraments, and every Confessor might demand of his Penitent whether he receivd it or rejected it; that a national Synod shou'd be assembled in order to examine the pastoral Letter of the Bishop of Aleth,†2 who had justify'd the Expulsion of the Jesuites; that the Jesuites shou'd be re-establish'd as an Order, useful to the Church and to the State; that all civil Tribunals shou'd be strictly prohibited to intermeddle in eclesiastical Affairs; and that Permission shou'd be granted to examine the Origin and Extent of the Primacy of Lions.
p 97
This Address was presented to the King, who reply'd, that he woud deliberate upon it; but he desir'd that the Clergy, mean-while, shoud proceed to the finishing of such other Matters as were the Objects of their being assembled. Tho' the Matter had been conducted with great Secrecy, the Parliament of Paris had receivd Intelligence of it; and they met on Friday was Sennight, in order to deliberate about it. After some Debate, it was agreed to wait the Resolution of the King. This Affair, tho' it may not be attended with any immediate and violent Consequence, is regarded as a Transaction of great Importance. The Opposition, which before subsisted, is renderd still more violent between the Robe and the Church; and it will soon, in all Probability prove difficult for the Crown to preserve Tranquillity between them: The most judicious of the Clergy are uneasy at these violent Proceedings. I was the first that gave an Account of the Address to the Bishop of Senlis,†1 a man of Sense and Knowlege; and he said to me, that the Matter was absolutely incredible, and that the whole must have been an Invention of the Enemies of the Clergy, in order [to] turn them into Ridicule. Mean-while, it is apparent from these and many other similar Events, that it is not likely France, were she disposd, will soon be in a Situation to disturb the Tranquillity of Europe.
p 98
The Dauphin†2 has been ill and confind to his bed these three days; but I do not find, that any body esteems his Case dangerous.
p 98
I am assurd that this Court is determind soon to acknowlege the King of Poland.†3 I have the Honour to be, with great Regard
Sir
Your most obedient & most humble Servant
DAVID HUME
Mr Secretary Conway
Letter 48: To H. M. SECRETARY OF STATE
p 98
†* 48. To [H. M. SECRETARY OF STATE]
Paris 18th of August 1765
Sir
As soon as I was honour'd with your Excellency's of the 10th Instant, which was not till Wednesday last, late in the Evening, I sent the enclos'd†4 to the Duke of Praslin, and ask'd his Directions, about the Delivery of His Majesty's Letters to the King and Queen of France. I have not as yet receiv'd any Answer; whence I conjecture, either that he has deliverd them himself, or waits till the Court returns from Compiegne, that I may attend him on that Occasion. I have the Honour to be, with the greatest Regard
Sir
Your Excellency's most obedient and most humble Servant
DAVID HUME
Letter 49: To the DUC DE PRASLIN

Paris 22me Août 1765
Monsieur Le Duc
J'ai l'honneur d'envoier le présent mémoire†1 à Votre Excellence en la priant de me faire dire quand je pourrai avoir celui de me rendre à ses ordres, afin de pouvoir conférer avec elle sur l'affaire dont il y est question.†2 Je désirerois fort, Monsieur Le Duc, que ce fût avant votre départ pour Versailles. J'ai l'honneur d'être, avec le plus profond respect,
Monsieur Le Duc
